# Log

Append-only, chronological. Prefix every entry `## [YYYY-MM-DD] <type> | <what>` so it stays greppable (`grep "^## \[" log.md | tail -5`).

## [2026-07-22] search | 2 candidates screened (from Zotero, not a fresh search), 0 included, 1 excluded, 1 staged

Follow-up to the same-day citation-accuracy check: two papers sitting in the user's Zotero library looked topically close to this review and were flagged for screening at the user's request, via the `wiki-search-enrich` skill rather than an ad hoc read. Both fetched and read in full (PMC mirror for Johansson, since MDPI blocked the direct fetch with a 403; Frontiers' own site for Hosoi), not screened from abstract or search-snippet alone.

- **Excluded**: Hosoi et al. (2023), *Frontiers in Neurology* 14:1219505 — minimal detectable change in the 10-meter walking test, stratified by gait speed. Confirmed via full text: measured with a digital stopwatch, no wearable sensor of any kind, and speed groups are manually thresholded (under 0.4 / 0.4-0.8 / above 0.8 m/s), not a data-driven classification method. Fails [[eligibility-criteria]]'s shared requirement across IC1/IC4/IC5 that a study involve wearable IMU-based gait methodology at all — not a borderline call.
- **Staged**: Johansson & Öhberg (2025), *Sensors* 25(10):3109 — augmented-feedback post-stroke gait rehabilitation case series. Genuinely uses 7 wearable IMUs on 4 real chronic stroke patients, extracting real spatiotemporal/kinematic features (confirmed via PMC full text), with no data-driven classifier — the same technical shape as [[felius-2024]]'s IC5 admission. But its own primary framing is a clinical-intervention case series, not a methodology evaluation the way IC5's three existing admits are, and n=4 with no within-study control group would make it a thin Table 3 addition even if included. Filed to `wiki/raw/johansson-2025-staged.md` rather than decided unilaterally — see that file for the full reasoning this needs a second look against.

No manuscript changes from this pass — neither candidate crossed the bar for inclusion yet.

**Real error found via the Zotero cross-check, then independently confirmed against Crossref directly** (never trust a single source for a fact going into the manuscript): the reference list cited the journal as "Gait and Posture" (spelled out) in three separate entries — [[jiao-2024]], [[da-silva-2024]], and the MAREA dataset citation (Khandelwal & Wickstrom, 2017) — when the journal's actual registered name, confirmed via a direct Crossref fetch on all three DOIs, is "**Gait & Posture**" (ampersand). Fixed all three reference-list entries. This had survived an earlier session's full reference-list DOI-verification pass, which checked that the DOIs resolved and were formatted as URLs but evidently didn't diff the exact journal-name string against Crossref's `container-title` field — worth remembering as a gap in what "DOI verified" actually covered previously.

**A second, independent error, found only because journal names being checked prompted a full title-string diff too**: [[avvenuti-2018]]'s reference-list title read "a single trunk *or* pocket-worn accelerometer," a paraphrase, while Crossref's `title` field for that DOI gives the actual published title as "a single trunk*/*pocket-worn accelerometer" (a slash, not "or"). APA style requires reproducing article titles exactly, not paraphrasing them. Fixed.

**Full sweep, not just the overlapping entries**: batch-fetched Crossref metadata (container-title, volume, page, title) for all 29 DOI-bearing references and diffed every one against the manuscript text (HTML-entity-decoded first, to avoid the `&amp;` vs `&` string-match false positive this same check kept tripping on). Zero further mismatches on container-title, volume, or page across all 29. One further title check flagged only the Avvenuti case above. The Oxford ORA dataset DOI (OxWalk) returned an expected Crossref 404 — already documented from an earlier session as one of the two entries (with the arXiv/GaitMotion preprint) Crossref doesn't index, verified directly against its own repository record instead.

(2023, *Frontiers in Neurology*, minimal detectable change in the 10-meter walk test for stroke) both look topically close to this review's scope and worth a screening pass if the user wants to expand the evidence base later — that's a [[wiki-search-enrich]] task, not something this citation-accuracy check should decide unilaterally. ## [2026-07-22] sync | Final QA pass: terminology-gloss check + formatting-consistency check, real bugs found in both

Same-day final round, two separate checks the user asked for explicitly. Ran `journal-format-check` (the project skill) for the submission-requirements side, and did a direct read-through plus run-level docx inspection myself for the rest, since "does every term get explained" isn't something a skill covers.

**Terminology gaps found and fixed** (special/technical terms used without explanation at first occurrence):
- "IMU" was used bare from Section 3.2.2 onward, only ever spelled out in full ("inertial measurement unit(s)") without the abbreviation ever being tagged to it, and only defined in the end-of-document abbreviations glossary — not at first occurrence in prose, per WIREs/APA style. Fixed by tagging "(IMUs)" onto the first full spelled-out use, in the Introduction.
- "Rank-biserial r" was reported with real numbers (Section 4.2.2) with no explanation that it's an effect-size measure, what scale it's on, or what it captures. Added a gloss at first occurrence.
- "Harmonic-locking" was used as an established term in Future Research Directions (item 5) but never actually named at its first description in Section 4.2.3 — the phenomenon was described in prose ("locked onto a spectrally stronger second harmonic") without ever being tagged with the term a later section assumes is already established. Fixed by naming it explicitly at first description.
- "Fugl-Meyer" and "Timed-Up-and-Go" (clinical assessment scale names, Section 2.2) were used with no explanation of what they measure — reasonable for a clinical journal, not for this journal's data-mining audience. Added a short parenthetical gloss for each at first occurrence.
- Checked and confirmed already adequate, no fix needed: sample entropy/harmonic ratio/Poincare SD1 (Table 6 already glosses these), cadence/RMS/stride-time/stride-time-CV (fixed earlier this session), Mann-Whitney U and ordinary least-squares regression (standard enough for this audience to leave unglossed), variational autoencoder (functionally explained even without architectural detail), PROBAST (positioned as a comparison point with enough context from its own citation).

**Formatting inconsistencies found and fixed** (run-level docx inspection, not just visual skim):
- **Line spacing**: the entire front half of the manuscript (title through early Methodology, ~48 paragraphs) had an explicit 1.15 multiple line-spacing override, while the rest of the body (126 paragraphs) had none (inheriting single spacing) — a real, visible split down the middle of the document. Normalized all Normal-style paragraphs to inherit consistently. Separately found one Heading 2 and six List Paragraph items as isolated outliers against their own style's dominant pattern and corrected each to match its peers (Heading 2 → inherited spacing to match its 17 siblings; the six List Paragraph items → explicit 1.15 to match their 29 siblings, since for that style 1.15 was the established majority convention, the reverse direction of the Normal-style fix).
- **A real bug from earlier today**: the 4.2.6 heading, created via `insert_paragraph_before()` when that section was first added, was missing the `space_before` value every other same-level heading has (the direct-formatting override doesn't automatically carry over from style-copying the way visual style does) — invisible unless you inspect paragraph_format directly, not something a visual read-through would necessarily catch. Fixed to match its 12 Heading-3 siblings.
- **Caption bold formatting, inconsistent across exactly half the tables**: Table 1, Table 2, and Figures 1-4 correctly used a bold "Table/Figure N. " prefix followed by a non-bold description (2 runs). Table 4 had *no* bold anywhere in its caption. Table 3, Table 5, and Table 6 had their *entire* caption bold, description included, not just the prefix. All four fixed to match the established 2-run pattern.
- **Reference-list italics**: today's own added Wang, J. et al. (2019) entry (Section 7's deep-learning justification citation) was written as a single unformatted run, missing the italicized "journal name, volume" that all 29 other reference entries carry per the Wiley style-manual fix from an earlier session. Fixed to match the exact 3-run split (author/year/title — italic journal+volume — issue/pages/DOI) already established.
- **Confirmed already consistent, no fix needed**: all 3 centered formula lines (font, italics, alignment identical), all 7 tables' header-row bold formatting and border styling (identical `w:tblBorders` values byte-for-byte across every table).

Both checks together caught six real, independently-discoverable defects — three formatting bugs traceable directly to this session's own earlier edits (the 1.15 spacing split's newer half, the 4.2.6 heading gap, the Wang 2019 reference italics) and three pre-existing gaps (Table 3/4/5/6 caption inconsistency, the IMU/rank-biserial/harmonic-locking/Fugl-Meyer terminology gaps) that had gone unnoticed until a dedicated pass looked for them specifically.

[[marea]], [[duo-gait]], [[gaitmotion]], [[camargo-2021]], [[oxwalk]] each had their pooled-reference bullet corrected to describe the surviving feature-level contribution and note, briefly, that a classifier-generalization version existed and was removed — so a future session finds the real history rather than wondering why a previously-logged false-positive-rate number disappeared. ## [2026-07-22] sync | Deep-learning future-investment justified explicitly, complementing Section 5.2's near-term verdict

Immediate follow-up to adding Section 5.2's classical-methods-favored verdict: the user asked how continued deep-learning investment should be justified given that verdict, correctly spotting that the existing Future Directions bullet (Section 7, item 6) only justified the *method-matched test* methodologically, not *why deep learning specifically still deserves it*. Strengthened with reasons already grounded in facts established elsewhere in the manuscript, not new claims: [[wang-2021]] already holds the highest accuracy of any included study using a deep model on raw signal with no hand-engineered features (a real ceiling, not just a caveat-laden result), and the data-efficiency concern is a dataset-*scale* problem this review's own seven-dataset audit shows is easing, not a fundamental capability limitation. Framed the two verdicts as complementary rather than contradictory: classical methods are the more plausible near-term choice on current evidence, deep learning remains worth testing properly before being ruled out. Applied to the manuscript (Section 7, item 6) and [[future-directions]].

## [2026-07-22] sync | Classifier-scope check confirmed, feature-meaning gloss added, explicit RQ3 verdict added to Section 5.2

Same-day follow-up. Two questions and one request:

Presented that history plus a relevance/performance/credibility assessment of each piece (Section 4.2.4: high value, more rigorous nested-CV design than most included literature; the new Section 4.2.6 feature comparison: high value, independently confirms the age-confound story; the classifier-generalization check specifically: scrutinized hardest since a subtler confound could hide in it, checked that age wasn't leaking in as a feature, found the highly non-uniform failure rate across datasets — 15% to 100% — was itself evidence it's tracking a real distributional gap rather than being a modeling artifact). User's own final call: decide based on relevance/performance/credibility rather than picking a preset option. Kept everything.

**Feature-meaning check**: user asked directly whether the manuscript explains what "good" discriminative features actually mean physically when first reported, worried it might not. Checked — it didn't, for the four basic features (cadence, mean stride time, stride-time variability, accelerometer RMS), though [[classification-methods]]'s Table 6 already correctly glosses the three literature-informed features (sample entropy, harmonic ratio, Poincare SD1) where they're introduced. Added a one-paragraph plain-language gloss for the four basic features immediately after the Section 4.2.2 heading, before any statistics use them. **RQ3 structuring**: user asked whether Section 5.2 should go further than describing the classical-vs-deep-learning pattern, toward an explicit verdict on which approach is more practically plausible right now versus lower priority — arguing this is what a genuinely strong Q1 systematic-review synthesis does with a literature comparison, not just report it. Agreed, and added one: classical methods are the more practically plausible near-term choice, not because deep learning is weaker in principle but because the current evidence doesn't support prioritizing it yet (best deep-learning result depends on a clean fixed placement, worst results come from the noisier placements this review's deployment interest actually targets, and 2 of 5 deep-learning studies don't attempt classification at all). Framed as promising-but-unproven, not lower-priority-on-merit, and tied directly to the method-matched comparison [[future-directions]] already recommends. Added to both the manuscript (new paragraph after Section 5.2's existing classical/DL comparison) and [[classification-methods]].

## [2026-07-22] sync | Section 4.2.6 rebuilt from scratch: pooled cross-dataset healthy reference, replacing the per-dataset-task framing the user correctly rejected

Same-day correction to the entry above. The user pushed back hard, twice: first that OxWalk had been left out ("I said already, I wanna use ALL datasets"), then more fundamentally that testing MAREA/DUO-GAIT/GaitMotion/Camargo against their own unrelated labeled tasks (environment, dual-task, locomotion mode) produced results that don't actually serve RQ1/RQ3 — "not helpful for getting pattern for stroke and distinguish them from healthy pp at all." Both objections were correct. This entry documents the full rebuild, not an addition alongside the old work — the old framing was removed from the manuscript entirely, not supplemented.

**New design**: every non-stroke dataset, OxWalk included this time, pooled into one 335-trial independent healthy reference (`src/features/cross_dataset.py`, new module), used two ways against the actual stroke-vs-healthy question:
1. **Feature-level (RQ1)**: do [[discriminative-features]]'s six shared discriminative features still separate pooled stroke (Voisard+Felius, n=192) from this pooled reference? Yes — every feature p < 1e-20, |r| = 0.62–0.88, all in the clinically expected direction. Also resolved a loose end: the broader reference's median cadence (~115) sits close to normal population values versus Voisard's own healthy controls (~78), independent confirmation that the original raw-cadence reversal (documented in [[discriminative-features]]) really was an artifact of Voisard's unusually young healthy sample, not a genuine effect.
2. **Classifier-level (RQ3)**: does the literature-informed classifier (trained on Voisard alone, or Felius alone) correctly call these 335 independent healthy people "healthy"? No — 70% (Voisard-trained) to 82% (Felius-trained) false-positive rate, varying hugely by source (15% for [[oxwalk]] vs. 100% for [[marea]] against the Voisard-trained classifier). Read together with check 1: the *features* generalize well, the *specific trained classifiers* do not transfer without recalibration — a real, nuanced, deployment-relevant distinction, not a simple failure.

**Two real bugs caught before any number was trusted**, both worth remembering since they're the kind of mistake easy to reintroduce:
- **Units mismatch**: an uncorrected first pass found 100.0% of all 335 healthy people classified as "stroke" with zero variation across datasets — too uniform to be real. Direct inspection found Voisard/MAREA/GaitMotion report accelerometer in m/s² while [[camargo-2021]]/[[duo-gait]]/[[oxwalk]]/[[felius-dataset]] report in g, and Voisard/[[camargo-2021]] report gyroscope in rad/s while [[duo-gait]]/[[gaitmotion]]/[[felius-dataset]] report in deg/s. Every raw signal is now converted to a canonical unit (g, deg/s) before any feature is derived.
- **Stride-time formula bug**: computed step time (60/cadence) where stride time (120/cadence, one full gait cycle = 2 steps) was needed — caught by comparing derived stride times against Voisard's own established convention, not by inspection alone.

Both bugs were caught by the same discipline this project applies everywhere else: never trust an intermediate result that looks suspiciously clean (a flat 100% with zero variation) without checking the raw numbers behind it.

**Manuscript**: Section 4.2.6 heading and all five body paragraphs replaced (was "Pipeline-Generalization Checks on Non-Stroke Datasets", now "Do the Discriminative Features and Classifier Generalize Beyond Voisard and Felius?"). Section 4.2.1's dataset-role sentence and the Conclusion's closing sentence on this topic both updated to match — the Conclusion previously said the pipeline "reached well-above-chance accuracy on all four" (the old per-dataset-task framing), now accurately describes the two-part feature-generalizes/classifier-doesn't-transfer finding.

**Notebook**: the old Part 4 (10 cells: MAREA/DUO-GAIT/GaitMotion/Camargo classification against their own tasks) deleted outright, not commented out or left alongside the new content. Replaced with a new Part 4 (10 cells: framing, pooled-reference build, feature comparison, classifier generalization check, synthesis) that imports and uses the new `cross_dataset` module. Full notebook re-executed via `nbconvert` after each of the two real bugs above was fixed, so the saved outputs reflect the corrected code, not the buggy first pass.

**Wiki**: [[classification-methods]] rewritten (not just appended to) to describe the new design and explicitly document why the first attempt was wrong. [[marea]], [[duo-gait]], [[gaitmotion]], [[camargo-2021]] each had their now-superseded per-dataset-task bullet replaced with the pooled-reference contribution, keeping one sentence noting what the removed finding was and why it was removed (so a future session doesn't wonder where a real, previously-logged 0.73/0.59/0.65/0.70 accuracy number went). [[oxwalk]] gets a genuinely new bullet, since it's included in the analysis for the first time. [[discriminative-features]] gets the cross-dataset validation result added directly, since it's really an RQ1 finding first and an RQ3 finding second.

## [2026-07-22] sync | Camargo unblocked, four new real classification analyses, RQ1-3 dataset distribution corrected

User pushback: only 2 of 7 hands-on-mined datasets ([[voisard-2025]], [[felius-dataset]]) were meaningfully contributing to RQ1/RQ3, and the user expected all seven to. Real hard constraint that couldn't be changed: RQ1/RQ3 are specifically about discriminating/classifying *stroke* gait, and [[duo-gait]], [[oxwalk]], [[marea]], and [[camargo-2021]] have zero stroke participants — no honest way to answer that question with them. What changed instead, after the user directly challenged the "Camargo is blocked" conclusion documented in this log's own 2026-07-17 and earlier entries:

**Camargo unblocked.** The prior "blocked" conclusion (scipy.io.loadmat and pymatreader both return only raw undocumented MCOS byte pointers for this dataset's MATLAB table-serialized files) was re-tested with a library neither this project nor that prior conclusion had tried: `mat-io` (`matio`), which correctly reconstructs MATLAB table objects as pandas DataFrames. Confirmed directly against decoded output, not assumed: sampling interval exactly 200 Hz, trunk accelerometer magnitude ~1.03g with plausible walking-scale variance, both matching the dataset's own documentation. All 22 subjects' already-downloaded data is now usable. New module: `src/features/camargo.py`. Added as a real dependency to `requirements.txt`.

**Four real classification analyses**, each a genuine new labeled task drawn from a dataset's own experimental design, explicitly *not* stroke evidence, using the same nested subject-grouped cross-validation + sequential backward feature selection pipeline built for Voisard/Felius in Section 4.2.4:
- [[camargo-2021]]: locomotion mode (level-ground/ramp/stair/treadmill), 3,147 trials, 22 subjects, 0.70 accuracy (SD 0.05), 4-class, chance 0.25. Also: four-placement comparison (trunk/thigh/shank/foot), 880 trials, corroborates [[duo-gait]]'s foot-highest/trunk-lowest ordering independently.
- [[marea]]: indoor vs. treadmill, 698 windows, 11 subjects, 0.73 (SD 0.02), 2-class. Deliberately excludes the outdoor segment — subjects 1-11 and 12-20 have zero overlap, so a 3-class version would let the classifier learn subject cohort instead of environment, a confound caught and avoided before running anything, not after.
- [[duo-gait]]: single-task vs. dual-task (raw signal only supports this split, not the fatigue dimension), 32 trials, 16 subjects, 0.59 (SD 0.09), near-chance, honestly reported as an underpowered near-null result rather than a negative finding.
- [[gaitmotion]]: simulated Normal/Parkinsonian/stroke, 460 trials (raw IMU, not just the pre-computed stride table), 10 subjects, 0.65 (SD 0.05), 3-class. Cadence and harmonic ratio deliberately excluded — GaitMotion's sampling rate could not be confirmed against the paper or the UBC repository, so no value was assumed.
- [[oxwalk]] deliberately left out — no non-trivial real labeled task exists beyond placement/demographics already used, and inventing one just to reach "all seven" would have been exactly the kind of forced, non-substantive use this whole expansion was trying to avoid.

All four results land clearly above chance and clearly below ceiling — checked explicitly for leakage-style red flags (e.g. the MAREA subject/environment confound) before trusting any number.

**Notebook**: all new code added as real cells (`src/features/camargo.py`, `src/features/marea.py::build_environment_classification_table`, `src/features/duogait.py::build_task_classification_table`, `src/features/gaitmotion.py::build_pathology_classification_table`, new Part 4 section), then the entire notebook re-executed end-to-end via `jupyter nbconvert --execute` rather than hand-copying scratch-run numbers into cells, so every output is authentically generated by the saved, re-runnable notebook. Fresh execution reproduced the validation-run numbers exactly (deterministic, fixed random seeds).

**Manuscript**: Section 4.2.1 intro updated (dataset roles). Section 4.2.5's Camargo paragraph rewritten from "excluded, unresolved" to the real placement finding. New Section 4.2.6 "Pipeline-Generalization Checks on Non-Stroke Datasets" inserted (old 4.2.6 "Remaining Gaps" renumbered to 4.2.7 — checked first for any other cross-references to "Section 4.2.6" by number elsewhere in the document; none existed outside the heading itself). Conclusion (Section 8) gets one added sentence. One real drafting error caught on self-review and fixed before finalizing: a cross-reference to "Section 4.1's MAREA case" that should have said "the MAREA check above" (MAREA is discussed two paragraphs earlier in this same new section, not in Section 4.1 at all).

**Wiki**: [[camargo-2021]] (existing page, not the duplicate `camargo.md` mistakenly created mid-session and then deleted once the existing page was found) rewritten in full. [[marea]], [[duo-gait]], [[gaitmotion]] each get a new finding bullet. [[sensor-placement]] and [[classification-methods]] updated with the corroborating placement finding and the pipeline-generalization summary respectively.

## [2026-07-22] sync | Figure 3 converted from an accuracy plot to a taxonomy diagram, after checking real precedent

Same-day follow-up to the Figure 2/3 linkage fix above. The user kept pushing on whether a plotted cross-study accuracy comparison could ever read as credible for this literature, and asked for actual research into how other systematic reviews handle it rather than continued self-directed redesign.

Checked the two closest published precedents directly (full texts already cached locally from earlier verification work, no new fetch needed): [[jiao-2024]] (21 studies, the same three-way stroke-vs-healthy / stroke-vs-other / stroke-subgroup-only split this manuscript uses) and [[boukhennoufa-2022]] (33 studies, three task categories). Neither plots accuracy across studies at all — both report per-study performance only in a table, and reserve actual figures for PRISMA flow diagrams, a taxonomy diagram, and a sensor-placement schematic. A supplementary web search on Cochrane diagnostic-test-accuracy methodology confirmed why a proper forest plot wasn't a good substitute either: it needs a confidence interval per study, which almost none of the included studies report. Separately, the manuscript's own Table 3 already had all seventeen studies' performance captured with full context (the plotted Figure 3 only covered 10, since it had to exclude studies reporting F1, AUROC, R², or timing error instead of plain accuracy) — so the plot was both non-standard for this literature and redundant with an existing table.

Given a choice between dropping Figure 3 entirely, replacing it with a non-comparison visual, restricting the plot to only the cleanest directly-comparable subset, or keeping it as-is, the user chose to replace it with a non-comparison visual, modeled on [[boukhennoufa-2022]]'s own taxonomy diagram.

**Built**: a hierarchical tree diagram (`visualizer` agent, briefed with the exact method-family/task-type classification for all 17 studies, derived directly from Section 4.1.3 and Table 3 rather than left to the agent) — root node "17 included studies," branching into Classical ML (10) / Deep Learning (5) / Threshold-based-no-trained-model (2), each further branching into task-type boxes (binary classification, multi-class/multi-group, fall-risk, regression, event-timing, unsupervised, event-detection) with individual study names as leaves, each leaf carrying its own specific comparison target (e.g. Hsu et al. (2018) reads "vs. other neuro. dx" right next to Hsu et al. (2021)'s "vs. healthy," so the shared "binary classification" bucket doesn't erase the distinction between them). No accuracy values anywhere on the figure. Deliberately built as a different visual form (tree, not grid or scatter) from every other figure in the manuscript, since it represents a genuinely different kind of information — structural/categorical, not per-study rated or measured.

**Manuscript changes**: Figure 3's intro paragraph and caption rewritten for the new content. Figure 2's intro paragraph, which had briefly cross-referenced Figure 3's (now nonexistent) accuracy figures, was corrected to point to Table 3 instead. Old Figure 3 image and its orphaned relationship cleaned up via the same zip-rebuild procedure used in every prior figure-swap this project (documented 2026-07-20, 2026-07-21, and earlier today) — python-docx does not garbage-collect a removed picture run's image part. [[classification-methods]] and [[quality-assessment]] updated with the full reasoning so a future session doesn't second-guess this back toward a plotted figure without re-deriving why it was changed.

## [2026-07-22] sync | Section 4-5 figure/notebook linkage fixes, two real fabricated numbers caught and corrected

User follow-up to the same-day abstract/prose pass: re-confirmed no sentence-initial "Yet" survives anywhere (this time checked all 8 tables and headers/footers too, not just body paragraphs — paragraph-only search would have missed table-cell text). Then addressed a substantive complaint: Section 4 onward felt fragmented, and Figures 1-3 seemed to force studies with genuinely different frameworks together without explaining why.

**Diagnosis** (from direct inspection of the actual embedded images, not just their captions): [[sensor-placement]]'s Figure 4 already handles study heterogeneity well — grouped rows, an explicit "Comparison target" column. [[quality-assessment]]'s Figure 2 didn't — 14 studies in one undifferentiated grid despite spanning classification/regression/event-timing tasks, and [[hsu-2021]]'s all-gray NA row had zero on-figure explanation (real reason: HTTP 403 blocked its full text everywhere, so nothing could be assessed — a disclosed Section 6 limitation, not a data gap). Figures 2 and 3 also never referenced each other despite rating overlapping studies, so a reader could see a high accuracy figure in Figure 3 with no visible link to that same study's weak risk-of-bias rating in Figure 2.

**Fixed directly in the manuscript**:
- Figure 2 regenerated (`visualizer` agent, dispatched with a fully-specified brief including the exact task-type label for each of the 14 studies, derived directly from Section 4 text rather than left to the agent to re-derive): rows now grouped under Classification (11) / Regression (2: [[sun-2025]], [[wu-2025]]) / Event-timing (1: [[lee-2025]]) headers, a new per-row "Task Type" column, and a footnote explaining [[hsu-2021]]'s all-gray row. Same color encoding and legend as before, unchanged.
- Figure 2 and Figure 3 intro paragraphs now explicitly cross-reference each other. Section 5's opening now states findings are weighed against their risk-of-bias rating rather than treated as uniformly reliable. Section 3.6 fixed one imprecise phrase ("trained classifier" → "trained classification or regression model" — [[wu-2025]] runs a regression, not a classifier, so the old phrasing didn't actually describe what it claimed to gate).
- Old Figure 2 image (`image2.png`) and its now-orphaned relationship (`rId6`) cleaned up via the same zip-rebuild procedure documented in this log's 2026-07-20 and 2026-07-21 entries — same recurring python-docx behavior, not a new bug.

**Also dispatched a `verifier` agent** (in parallel with the `visualizer` work, per the user's explicit go-ahead to use subagents here) to mechanically cross-check every number in Section 4.2 against the actual notebook (`notebooks/01_post_stroke_gait_baseline.ipynb`) cell outputs — the "does the notebook actually back up what Section 4.2 claims" question. Ten of twelve checked items confirmed clean. Two were real, and per this project's "verify a subagent's own fix before trusting it" discipline, both were independently re-checked directly against the notebook before touching the manuscript:
- **Fabricated numeric range** in the MAREA signal-processing-failure paragraph (manuscript paragraph 148): claimed "225 to 270 steps per minute" (naive) and "110 to 135 steps per minute" (corrected) for the Fourier-peak-picking failure. Neither range appears in any notebook cell output — only single point estimates (~246, ~123) exist, in markdown prose, for a *different* nearby finding (the waist-channel step-vs-stride conversion bug). Fixed by removing the fabricated ranges and describing the Fourier-peak-picking failure qualitatively, consistent with what Section 3.8 already says about it. ## [2026-07-20] setup | Skill Map, templates, and raw/ staging area added

Expanded `CLAUDE.md` from a basic three-workflow schema into a full Skill Map, adapting Nick Milo's Obsidian + Claude Cowork "AIOS" video (*How I Use Obsidian + Claude Cowork to Run My Life*) to Claude Code's session-based execution model. Cowork's scheduled, multi-source (Gmail/Calendar/ClickUp) automation has no direct equivalent here — the adaptation is a fixed menu of six named, on-request skills instead of a timed daily brief:

1. **Ingest** — file a new, already-identified study/dataset/finding (pre-existing, now formalized under the Skill Map).
2. **Sync** (new) — detect when the manuscript, search log, or notebook changed and propagate the change into the wiki. Directly addresses the "keep observing changes and apply changes to codespace and drafts" requirement — on-request rather than continuous, since Claude Code has no background file-watcher by default.
3. **Source Search & Enrichment** (new) — proactively search for new literature via WebSearch/WebFetch, screen against `concepts/eligibility-criteria.md`, and either Ingest, stage in `raw/`, or log as excluded. Formalizes the same process the two background agents ran earlier this session (which found Ensink, resolved Werner, and excluded Sadeghsalehi) into a repeatable, documented skill.
4. **Draft Assist** (new) — use the wiki as the knowledge source when writing or revising the actual manuscript, rather than re-deriving synthesis from scratch. Carries forward the manuscript's own standing prose rules (no semicolons, no parenthetical Section refs, short connector-driven sentences).
5. **Query** — pre-existing, now formalized under the Skill Map.
6. **Lint** — pre-existing, now formalized under the Skill Map, extended to also check `raw/` for staged items aging without resolution.

Also added: a session-start protocol (paste-able prompt combating model amnesia between sessions, analogous to the video's "SSK" hotkey), model-selection guidance adapted to Claude Code's `/model` switching and the `Agent` tool's per-call `model` parameter, `templates/` (one starter file per page type: study, dataset, concept, review), and `raw/README.md` establishing the staging-inbox convention.

No wiki content pages were changed in this pass — this was infrastructure only.

Confirmed via research (Claude Code docs) that a nested `wiki/CLAUDE.md` only auto-loads once Claude opens a file inside `wiki/` during a session — it does *not* load automatically at session start if the working directory is the project root. Fixed by adding `../CLAUDE.md` at the project root, which does auto-load every session regardless of starting directory.

## [2026-07-20] correction | journal-format-check rebuilt on the actual primary source

The user correctly called out that `journal-format-check` was built from WebSearch summaries, not an actual document. Fixed: fetched Wiley's real Journals Style Manual (v5.5, 2023-12-01) directly from `https://authors.wiley.com/asset/Wiley-Journals-Style-Manual.pdf`. WebFetch's own text extraction failed on the PDF (returned binary noise), so the raw file was downloaded and extracted locally with `pypdf` instead — 196 pages, saved permanently at `.claude/skills/journal-format-check/reference/wiley_journals_style_manual_v5.5_extracted.txt` so future sessions don't need to re-fetch.

This is a real primary source, but it's Wiley's *general* cross-journal house style, not WIREs-DMKD-specific — its own generic article-type list doesn't include "Systematic Review" OR any of WIREs' own category names, confirming WIREs runs a separate category system this document doesn't cover. The APA reference formatting, abstract rules, and keyword rules it does cover are real Wiley house style and apply here. One concrete finding from actually applying the real keyword rule against the manuscript: the current Keywords line isn't alphabetized and starts with a capital letter, both violating the verified rule — flagged, not yet fixed (pending user confirmation, since keyword wording itself is an editorial choice).

## [2026-07-20] search | Real precedent, PRISMA writing guidance, and lit-review academic guidance added

Three further real sources found and applied, per user request to look beyond the single style-manual PDF:

1. **Real WIREs DMKD precedent for "Systematic Review"**: Calegari & Delgado (2025), *A Systematic Review on Process Mining for Curricular Analysis*, WIREs Data Mining and Knowledge Discovery 15(2):e70005, https://doi.org/10.1002/widm.70005 — confirmed via a WIREs Online Library search hit and independently via Crossref metadata (journal-article, container-title matches, published April 2025). This is real, dated, checkable evidence the journal accepts and publishes work explicitly titled/framed as a systematic review, on top of the user's own direct statement. Structural comparison data pulled from its arXiv preprint (arxiv.org/abs/2409.09204): ~190-word abstract, 4 keywords, Introduction → Literature Review Methodology → Data Synthesis & Results → Opportunities & Challenges → Threats to Validity → Conclusions.
2. **PRISMA 2020 Explanation & Elaboration** (Page et al., 2021, BMJ, PMC8005925) — item 13's synthesis-methods guidance and item 5's precision-of-exclusion-language guidance, both checked directly against the actual paper (not a summary).
3. **University academic-writing-center guidance on PhD thesis literature reviews** (Sheffield, Kent) — thematic-not-chronological structure, "synthesize across sources rather than describing them one by one," "sources are evidence for your argument," and the synthesis-matrix planning tool, which `wiki/concepts/` pages already structurally implement.

All three folded into `.claude/skills/journal-format-check/SKILL.md` (precedent) and `.claude/skills/wiki-draft-assist/SKILL.md` (writing guidance), with real citations, not paraphrased search snippets.

(2025), *Applied Sciences* 15(22):11974, was in the reference list but never cited in the manuscript body, never appears in the search log's screening records under any stage (scoping, formal round 1, formal round 2), and is off-topic for this review (smartphone gait detection for sports medicine, no stroke content). " prefix + Pt10 for both runs — for all three figure captions, matching `Figure 1.`'s caption once that was added too).

## [2026-07-22] sync | CVA gloss and a verified (not assumed) explanation for the harmonic-ratio/Poincare-SD1 disagreement

The user asked two direct questions after the previous round: what CVA actually means (noting it wasn't called out inline in the manuscript prose, only in the abbreviations table), and whether a real clinical difference between Voisard's "CVA" cohort and Felius's "Stroke" cohort could be the cause of the sample-entropy/harmonic-ratio/Poincare-SD1 direction disagreement rather than a hardware/orientation artifact — then explicitly asked to verify this wasn't a coding bug before writing anything.

**Coding-bug check (verified, no bug found)**: read `src/features/signal_utils.py`, `voisard.py`, and `felius.py` directly. Both datasets call the identical `sample_entropy`/`harmonic_ratio`/`poincare_sd` functions with the same parameters (same magnitude-based approach, same default axis convention) — no per-dataset formula divergence. Sample entropy self-normalizes by each signal's own SD and harmonic ratio is a same-signal amplitude ratio, so both are scale-invariant — the known Voisard/Felius gyroscope-unit difference (rad/s vs deg/s) can't explain a direction reversal either. Confirmed with the notebook's actual cached numeric output (cells 53/55/58): Voisard CVA > HS on all three features (0.609 vs 0.321 sample entropy, 1.037 vs 0.900 harmonic ratio, 0.081 vs 0.061 Poincare SD1), Felius Stroke < Healthy on harmonic ratio and Poincare SD1 (2.781 vs 4.844, 2.509 vs 5.156) with a near-null sample-entropy difference (0.996 vs 1.028) — a real, verified reversal, not a bug artifact.

**CVA definition, verified directly**: `data/raw/voisard_2025/data/neuro/CVA/CVA_1/CVA_1_1/CVA_1_1_meta.json` has `"pathology": "cerebrovascular accident"`, `"pathologyKey": "CVA"` — confirms CVA = cerebrovascular accident, the standard clinical synonym for stroke, matching the manuscript's own abbreviations-table entry. That table entry already existed, but the term was never glossed inline where first used in Section 4.2.2's prose (para 137). Fixed: para 137 now reads "the 49 CVA (cerebrovascular accident, Voisard's own stroke-cohort label) and 73 healthy (HS) participants."

**Whether a clinical cohort difference explains the reversal — genuinely unresolvable, and now with direct evidence why**: this was checked exhaustively rather than assumed. Voisard's own raw metadata does carry real per-patient clinical variables (`clinicalDeficitSide`, `evaluationScoreName: "FMA-LE"` with a real severity score, TUG) — confirmed directly in the same meta.json file, currently underused per [[future-directions]]. Felius provides none. To confirm this wasn't just an incomplete local download, downloaded the complete public Zenodo archive fresh (record 11045239, `RichardFel/VAE-V1.0.6.zip`, 415MB, all 1,191 files) and searched every file path for any clinical/demographic keyword (age, sex, severity, FAC, fugl, chronic, acute, onset, clinical, patient, demograph, character, ontslagen, lesion, hemi, fma) — zero matches, confirming our local extraction already matches the full public release exactly. But `Functions/Progression.py`, inside that same public archive, contains `pd.read_excel('Data/Characteristics/ontslagen.xlsx')` ("ontslagen" = Dutch for "discharged") — direct proof a participant-characteristics file existed for the original authors' own analysis and was never included in the public release, rather than never having been collected at all.

**Manuscript changes**: Section 5.1's disagreement paragraph (para 179) expanded from two candidate explanations (hardware/mounting/protocol, orientation-invariant magnitude computation) to three, adding the clinical-cohort-difference possibility with the specific `ontslagen.xlsx` evidence for why it can't be tested. Section 4.2.2's para 137 gained the inline CVA gloss. Document integrity confirmed unchanged (256 paragraphs, 7 tables, 4 inline shapes).

**Consequence for the wiki**: `datasets/felius-dataset.md` gained the specific `ontslagen.xlsx` finding (replacing the vaguer "no demographic data accompanies the public release" framing) and the three-explanations list for the direction disagreement. `concepts/discriminative-features.md`'s disagreement bullet updated to match. Not yet addressed, flagged for a future pass if the user wants it: Section 4.2.4's para 158 currently names only sample entropy and Poincare SD1 as disagreeing in direction, omitting that harmonic ratio does too (confirmed via the same notebook cell 56/58 output above) — raised to the user in conversation but not yet actioned pending explicit confirmation.

**Correction, same day**: the entries above and in `datasets/felius-dataset.md` initially cited the `ontslagen.xlsx` evidence by its local in-archive path only. The user pointed out a citation like that should point to something publicly checkable, not a path only meaningful inside this local environment. Verified and added the real public sources: the Zenodo record itself is https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.11045239, and the exact code file proving the characteristics file existed is publicly viewable, unaltered, at https://github.com/RichardFel/VAE/blob/V1.0.6/Functions/Progression.py (confirmed via direct fetch that this URL shows the same `pd.read_excel('Data/Characteristics/ontslagen.xlsx')` line). `datasets/felius-dataset.md` updated to cite these directly rather than the local path.

## [2026-07-28] sync | Placement-neutrality pass: removed advocacy framing around pocket-carried sensors, new reusable skill built

User flagged that every statement touching pocket-carried sensor placement, across the Abstract, Discussion, and Conclusion, read as the review pushing toward a predetermined conclusion (that pocket placement is the right answer) rather than reporting findings neutrally, "not like i am forcing the study into my intended direction."

**Root-cause read**: grepped every paragraph mentioning "pocket" or trunk/back/shank/thigh placement (11 paragraphs) plus every occurrence of "motivating interest," "low-burden," and "consumer-style" (9 paragraphs) and compared against Section 1's actual framing, which treats placement as one of three neutrally-weighted threads with no stated preference. The bias had crept in only during Discussion/Conclusion drafting in earlier sessions, not from the Introduction's own framing. Concrete patterns found, all inconsistent with the evidence actually available (2 stroke-specific pocket-placement studies, Shin 2022 and Lee et al. 2025, versus 5 studies converging on trunk placement):
- **"This review's motivating interest in low-burden, consumer-style wearables"** (paras 115, 169, 190, 216) — stated the review had a preferred outcome before the evidence, rather than a neutral research question.
- **Overclaiming strength from thin evidence**: "establish...not merely theoretically possible" (para 32, 3 small studies), "confirm" (para 115, 2 studies), "it does" as settled fact (para 191, 2 studies) — verbs implying certainty the sample size didn't support, while trunk placement's 5-study convergence never got equivalently strong language.
- **"Narrower than this review expected going in"** (para 212, Conclusion) — revealed a prior hope/expectation being confirmed, rather than a neutral empirical result.
- **A normative product-design recommendation** ("Consider a tiered deployment instead," para 191) stated as a proposal rather than a hypothesis, when no included study had tested any tiered protocol.
- **Editorializing** ("not a compromise too costly to consider," para 215) nudging the reader toward accepting pocket placement rather than stating the trade-off neutrally.

**Fixed directly in the manuscript** (paras 4, 32, 51, 115, 169, 190, 191, 212, 215, 216 — verified 256 paragraphs/7 tables/4 inline shapes unchanged before and after): every "motivating interest" phrase removed and replaced with placement-neutral language ("a placement class of practical interest for... deployment"), overclaiming verbs downgraded to match evidence strength ("establish"→"indicate," "confirm"→"indicate," "it does"→"the two studies indicate it can") with the underlying study count stated explicitly alongside each claim, the tiered-deployment paragraph reframed as an explicitly untested hypothesis rather than a recommendation, "expected going in" and "not a compromise too costly to consider" removed, and the abstract/conclusion's comparison ordering changed to trunk-first/pocket-second throughout to match where the stronger evidence actually sits.

**Wiki**: [[placement-vs-practicality]] rewritten to match — added an explicit "Bias correction, 2026-07-28" note at the top (so a future session doesn't wonder why the framing changed) and reworded the tension/reframe sections the same way as the manuscript. [[sensor-placement]] and [[future-directions]] checked and already neutral, no changes needed.

**New reusable skill**: built `~/.claude/skills/reviewer-neutrality-check` (global, not project-scoped), since the user asked for this to be usable by other projects, not just this one. Generalizes the diagnostic method used here (grep for the review's own stated "interest"/"motivation" language, check strong verbs against actual sample sizes, check whether forward-looking proposals are labeled as hypotheses versus findings) into a repeatable procedure for any systematic-review or synthesis-writing project.

## [2026-07-28] sync | Same-day follow-up: the real complaint was structural, not word-choice — abstract reordered, skill extended

Immediate follow-up to the entry above. The user pushed back that the word-choice fixes still missed the actual problem: in the Abstract, the sentence "A pocket-carried consumer-style sensor remains usable..." sat directly after a sentence about classical-versus-deep-learning method comparison, with no logical connection to it at all — the pocket-placement claim read as dropped in wherever there was room for it, not argued to, even though every word in it was now neutral. This is a distinct failure mode from the word-choice bias fixed in the previous pass: a *structural* non-sequitur, where a favored claim has no motivating link to the sentence immediately before it.

**Fixed in the manuscript**: the Abstract (paragraph 4) reordered so both placement findings, trunk-placement-as-discriminator and pocket-placement-as-usable-alternative, sit adjacent to each other as one continuous thread, with an explicit bridge ("Tested directly against that same trunk-placement standard,..."), followed by the method-comparison thread introduced with its own transition ("On classification method,..."), before the closing future-directions sentence that ties both threads together. Previously the order interleaved placement → method → placement with no signal that the topic had switched back. Verified 256 paragraphs/7 tables/4 inline shapes unchanged. Checked the Discussion (Section 5.3) and Conclusion paragraphs covering the same placement material for the same structural problem — found none, since those sections have dedicated headed paragraphs for placement and for method separately, so the topic switch is signaled by the heading rather than needing a same-paragraph bridge. The Abstract is uniquely exposed to this failure mode because it is the one place in the manuscript that compresses every thread into a single paragraph with no headings to do that signaling work.

**Skill extended**: added a seventh marker category to `~/.claude/skills/reviewer-neutrality-check/SKILL.md`, "abrupt, unmotivated topic insertion (structural, not word-level)" — read each sentence against the one immediately before it and ask what specifically motivates it; if the answer is "nothing, the previous sentence was on a different topic," the claim was dropped in rather than derived. Documented that this is most likely to surface in densely compressed sections (abstracts, executive summaries, a conclusion's synthesis paragraph) that lack headings, and that the fix is reordering to group same-topic sentences adjacently, not just adding a transition phrase to paper over the gap. Procedure section updated to run this sentence-by-sentence read as a separate pass from the word-choice grep, since it can catch bias with zero flagged words.

**Same-day correction to the skill itself**: the user pushed back again — checking only the single sentence immediately before a claim is too rigid, since a claim can be legitimately grounded by something stated earlier in the same paragraph, or by a whole heading-scoped section's stated purpose, not just its literal predecessor. Revised marker 7 to pick the correct governing unit first (the whole paragraph for undifferentiated prose like an abstract or a conclusion's synthesis paragraph, the whole heading-scoped section for text already organized under topic headings) and check the claim against that unit as a whole, rather than a fixed one-sentence lookback. Re-verified the abstract reordering above against this corrected rule and confirmed it still holds, no further manuscript change needed this pass.

## [2026-07-28] sync | New skill built: academic-tone-check, manuscript audited for casual wording

User asked for a dedicated skill checking that only academic-register wording is used throughout the manuscript, flagging casual terms — contractions, informal connectives like "yet"/"so" used as filler — beyond what any other project skill already covers (`journal-format-check` covers citation/structure, `reviewer-neutrality-check` covers advocacy framing, neither checks word-level formality).

**New global skill built**: `~/.claude/skills/academic-tone-check/SKILL.md` (global, matching the `reviewer-neutrality-check` precedent, since tone/register checking is generically useful beyond this project). Covers contractions, casual sentence-initial connectives ("Yet," "So," "Well," "Now," as stand-alone transitions), vague intensifiers ("really," "very," "basically," "actually" as filler), colloquial phrasal verbs ("figure out," "look into," "get" as a general-purpose verb), and conversational fillers, with explicit guidance not to flag a word's legitimate ordinary use just because the same word has a casual use elsewhere (e.g. "not yet available" is fine, only a sentence-initial transitional "Yet" is not).

**Applied immediately to the manuscript**: regex-audited every paragraph and table cell for contraction patterns and the listed casual markers. Result: **zero actual verb contractions anywhere** — every `'s`/`'re` hit the audit's first-pass regex caught turned out to be a genuine possessive ("this review's," "Voisard's," "Felius's," "Parkinson's disease") or a genitive in a study title, not a contraction, so nothing needed fixing there. Sentence-initial "Yet" was already removed in an earlier session's pass (2026-07-22 log entry) and stayed clean. The one genuine, actionable finding: **eight instances of a mid-sentence causal "so"** ("X, so Y") in paragraphs 88, 94, 145, 175, 177 (two), 184, and 197 — a casual-register connector inconsistent with this project's own standing sentence-style rule in `CLAUDE.md` ("short, moderate-length sentences joined with explicit connectors: therefore, however, as a result"). All eight split into two sentences with "therefore" as the connecting adverb, preserving every fact and citation exactly. Verified 256 paragraphs/7 tables/4 inline shapes unchanged, and re-ran the audit after editing to confirm zero mid-sentence causal "so" instances remain.

**Not changed**: "actually," "well," "just," and "so far"/"so that" instances were checked individually and left alone — each one is a legitimate adverbial, restrictive, or subordinating use in its actual sentence (e.g. "performing well," "not just a reported accuracy figure," "available so far"), not casual filler, and the skill itself warns against reflexively swapping a fine word for an equally vague replacement just to look more aggressively edited.

Added `academic-tone-check` to root `CLAUDE.md`'s global-skills list alongside `ai-research-rigor` and `reviewer-neutrality-check`, with a note on when to reach for it (before any submission-track milestone).

User flagged a real gap none of the tone-check passes above had ever touched: the manuscript's own pre-existing standing rules already ban semicolons entirely and prefer moderate-length sentences joined by explicit connectors over long comma-separated chains, but no pass this session had actually swept the document for either.

Verified zero semicolons remain anywhere in the document, tables included.

**Comma-chain sentences**: wrote a heuristic sentence-splitter (splitting on `.?!` before a capital letter, protecting "et al." and similar abbreviations from false splits) and counted commas per sentence across every body paragraph, excluding the reference list (author-name lists are inherently comma-heavy and not a style problem). The real, fixable pattern, several independent facts strung together with commas and no word stating their relationship, was found in 12 sentences (paragraphs 4 [×2], 48, 71, 94, 130, 132, 138, 160, 162, 166, 213 [×2]) and each was split into two sentences at the point the logical relationship changed, adding a connector ("however," "since," "This is") only where the relationship needed stating explicitly. Verified 256 paragraphs/7 tables/4 inline shapes unchanged, and re-ran both the semicolon and comma-density sweeps after editing to confirm the fixes landed and nothing else regressed.

**Skill extended**: added marker 9 to `~/.claude/skills/academic-tone-check/SKILL.md`, "sentence-structure markers — semicolons and comma-chain density," covering both findings as a single, previously-missing check category. Documented the list-versus-chain distinction explicitly, since over-fixing a genuine list would make prose worse, not better, and added the rule that semicolon checks must include table cells and captions, not only body paragraphs, since that is exactly where this pass found six real violations a paragraph-only grep had missed.

## [2026-07-28] sync | Abstract re-checked by hand, comma-count threshold replaced with a per-comma job test

User pushed further, asking directly whether the abstract itself passed this check and what the actual criteria was — a fair challenge, since the comma-count-based sweep above had only fixed sentences crossing an arbitrary threshold (6+) without verifying the *reasoning*, not just the count, held up under scrutiny. Manually re-read every sentence in the Abstract (paragraph 4) and found three genuine structural problems the count-based pass had missed or left half-fixed:

- **Sentence 3**: "however" sat in the wrong spot, attached to the participial phrase "covering X" instead of the main clause it was meant to modify. Moved to directly after the subject ("This literature, however, has developed...") — the grammatically correct placement, even though this raised the sentence's own comma count from 5 to 6.
- **Sentence 6**: "although at a real accuracy cost" is not a grammatical clause — "although" needs a subject and verb, not a bare prepositional phrase. Removed "although" and folded the phrase into the main clause.
- **Sentence 7**: three separate insertions (a redundant topic tag, a participial aside, an appositive) were stacked before the sentence ever reached its own verb. Cut the redundant opener and converted the trailing participial clause into a properly coordinated verb phrase ("dominates... and reaches...").

Verified 256 paragraphs/7 tables/4 inline shapes unchanged. Re-checked the Abstract's remaining high-comma sentences (6, 5, and 4 commas respectively) against a stated, explicit test rather than the count alone: does every comma in the sentence do one of exactly three jobs — separate parallel list items, bracket one aside/appositive, or mark a clause boundary right before a real coordinating word ("and," "but")? All three passed this test even at their comma counts, confirming the count was never the actual signal, only a rough filter for where to look closer.

**Skill corrected once more**: `~/.claude/skills/academic-tone-check/SKILL.md` marker 9 rewritten so the comma count is explicitly framed as a filter for where to look, not the actual pass/fail test. Added the three-job rule (list / aside / marked clause boundary) as the real test, plus two specific sub-patterns this review surfaced: a connector word ("although," "though," "while") attached to a bare phrase instead of a real clause is a chain problem regardless of the sentence's overall comma count, and a misplaced parenthetical adverb ("however," etc.) is a distinct bug from comma-chaining that can legitimately *increase* the comma count when fixed, since correct grammatical attachment matters more than a lower number.

## [2026-07-28] sync | The comma-splitting pass had a side effect: some new sentence breaks lost their connective tissue

User caught a direct consequence of the comma-density fixes above: splitting a sentence to get under the 3-comma ceiling removes whatever word (a comma, "and," "while") used to link the two halves, and if nothing replaces it, the resulting second sentence can read as a cold restart rather than a continuation, even though each half is individually clean.

**Checked the abstract specifically** (the user's own example) sentence-by-sentence for this: most of the splits from the prior pass already carry their own link forward via a referential word ("those datasets," "this same feature evidence," "on this basis") or an existing connector ("however," "instead"), so no fix was needed there. One genuine gap: "This literature, however, has developed along three separate tracks... None of these tracks connects into a single account..." — the second sentence had no explicit link. Added "Critically," to its start. Also lightly touched paragraph 48 (the classifier-prevalence sentence split earlier this pass), adding "Meanwhile," to its second half for the same reason, as a parallel-structure sentence that could still read a shade smoother with an explicit marker.

**Answered the user's actual question directly**: an abstract does still need this connective tissue, its density doesn't exempt it, but that does not mean every sentence needs its own transition word — only the specific joins where the logical link isn't already obvious from a referential word or the sentence's own structure. Over-adding a connector to every sentence would pad the abstract without adding information, which is its own register problem in the other direction.

**Skill extended**: added a bullet to marker 9 in `~/.claude/skills/academic-tone-check/SKILL.md` — after any comma-driven sentence split, re-check whether the new second sentence still flows from the first, and fix with either a short connector (when the relationship needs stating explicitly) or nothing (when a referential word already carries the link). This is now a required step immediately after applying the comma-cap fix, not a separate pass to remember later.

## [2026-07-29] sync | journal-critic major finding 3.4 fixed: RQ2 trunk-placement convergence overclaim

The manuscript repeatedly stated "five included studies converge on the same placement" as support for trunk/lower-back placement (Abstract, Section 5.3, Conclusion). `journal-critic` flagged this as overclaiming: four of the five ([[mannini-2016]], [[hsu-2021]], [[lee-2018]], [[inui-2026]]) tested only a single placement each and never ran a comparison, so their "performing well independently" is not comparative evidence for trunk over an alternative. Only [[abdollahi-2024]] ran a genuine comparison in this direction, and it favors a thorax sensor, a nearby but distinct site from lumbar/lower back, not the same one. Meanwhile the two other genuine literature multi-placement stroke comparisons, [[hsu-2018]] and [[pohl-2022]], do not favor trunk at all, and [[brasiliano-2026]] (the largest multi-placement comparison in the review) never reports which placement produced its retained features -- a real, unresolved reporting gap this review cannot fill from the published text.

Rewrote the Abstract, Section 5.3's main convergence paragraph, and the Conclusion's matching paragraph to state plainly that trunk placement's advantage is well supported by this review's own re-mining (a genuine multi-placement comparison on [[voisard-2025]] and [[felius-dataset]]) and by one literature comparison in a nearby site, but is not the uniform verdict of the multi-placement literature as a whole -- rather than counting single-placement studies as convergence. [[sensor-placement]] rewritten to match, restructuring the old "trunk-placement convergence" section into a corrected "trunk-placement evidence" section that distinguishes comparative from non-comparative evidence explicitly and names the Brasiliano provenance gap for the first time.

## [2026-07-29] sync | journal-critic major findings 3.5 and 3.8 fixed: participant-level re-analysis and age-collinearity check

**3.5 (trial-vs-participant non-independence)**: the Section 4.2.6 cross-dataset validation reported n=192 stroke and n=335 healthy, both trial-level counts that treat repeated trials from the same person as independent observations -- exactly the sampling flaw this review's own Section 2.1 credits [[jiao-2024]] with identifying as the field's weakest methodological criterion. Checked directly against the real underlying data rather than assumed: Voisard contributes 128 CVA trials from 49 unique participants, Felius 64 Stroke trials from 34, and the five-dataset healthy reference 335 trials from only 106 unique people, with Camargo alone contributing up to 5 trials per subject and GaitMotion 150 trials from just 10 subjects. Rather than only softening the manuscript's wording, re-ran the actual comparison at the participant level (one row per subject, mean across that subject's trials) using the project's own `cross_dataset.discriminative_feature_comparison` function -- the real, corrected result is 83 stroke vs 107 healthy participants, every feature still significant at p < 1e-5 (previously <1e-20), effect sizes |r| = 0.44-0.69 (previously 0.62-0.88). The effect survives properly-independent testing, so this became a genuine strengthening of the manuscript's rigor rather than a retraction. Fixed in the manuscript (Section 4.2.6 and the Conclusion), the notebook (`01_post_stroke_gait_baseline.ipynb` cells 67-69, code and output both re-run for real, not hand-edited numbers), and the wiki ([[discriminative-features]], [[classification-methods]]).

**3.8 (age-residualization collinearity concern)**: `journal-critic` questioned whether Voisard's ~21-year HS-vs-CVA age gap makes age and group membership too collinear for linear residualization to cleanly separate an age effect from a genuine pathology effect (the classic Lord's-paradox risk). Quantified directly rather than argued in the abstract: point-biserial r = 0.48 between age and group, VIF approximately 1.3 -- moderate correlation, not severe by conventional VIF thresholds (5-10), and every CVA participant's age falls within the HS group's own broader range, meaning real within-group age variation exists for the regression to use. Added to the manuscript's Section 4.2.2 and to [[voisard-2025]] as a disclosed, quantified check rather than an unaddressed assumption.

## [2026-07-29] fix | third journal-critic pass: Mannini et al. (2016) mischaracterization corrected (fatal finding)

A third, independent journal-critic adversarial review flagged that Mannini et al. (2016) has been miscited throughout the manuscript as evidence of "lumbar-spine sensor performing well independently," used since well before this session as one leg of the trunk-placement convergence argument. Verified directly against the full text (PMC): Mannini's seven-variable HMM feature vector draws six variables from a single shank sensor and only one (mediolateral acceleration) from the waist. Removing Mannini leaves no genuinely independent third-party literature corroboration for the trunk-placement finding at all -- disclosed plainly in the manuscript rather than papered over.

Fixed throughout: Section 4.1.2 (para 105), Section 4.2.5 (para 157), Section 5.1 (para 173), Section 5.3 (para 190, now listing three studies not four, with Mannini's exclusion explained), and the Conclusion (para 214, count corrected from four to three). ## [2026-07-30] fix | fourth journal-critic pass: two fatal findings investigated and resolved, nine major findings fixed

A fourth `journal-critic` adversarial pass, dispatched fresh (not a rubber stamp) after the third pass's fixes were applied, found two genuinely new fatal-level issues plus nine major findings, all investigated and fixed this session, none deferred except the already-known PRISMA-reproducibility question.

**FATAL-1, investigated and resolved**: the third pass's own "cadence and mean stride time are mathematically related by definition, since Table 6 defines stride time as 120 divided by cadence" claim was itself wrong. Direct re-computation against the raw feature-engineering code (`src/features/voisard.py`, `src/features/cross_dataset.py`) found stride time is measured independently from detected gait events for Voisard/Felius, not derived from cadence, correlating at Pearson r ≈ -0.60, about 10% off the idealized reciprocal value in typical magnitude — not an exact identity. This means the age-adjustment divergence reported in Section 4.2.2 (cadence loses significance at p = .315, stride time survives at r = -0.43) is a genuine finding, not a residualization artifact on a reciprocal transform. Table 6's formula was corrected, and Section 4.2.6's "four effectively independent quantities" framing was replaced with an honest correlation disclosure. A genuine asymmetry was also found and disclosed: the pooled *healthy-reference* datasets (unlike Voisard/Felius) do compute stride time as exactly 120/cadence, since no independently labeled gait event exists for those signals.

**FATAL-2, investigated and resolved**: the pooled cross-dataset comparison in Section 4.2.6 pools GaitMotion's Normal condition into the healthy reference tested against stroke, contradicting three separate "never pooled" statements elsewhere in the manuscript (Sections 3.8, 4.2.1, 4.2.5). Those statements were rescoped to explicitly disclose the Section 4.2.6 exception. More substantively, direct re-computation added a per-healthy-dataset sensitivity check: sample entropy holds up against every individual healthy comparator dataset (p < .001 in each case), but trunk RMS is not significant against OxWalk alone and cadence/stride time are not significant against DUO-GAIT alone, though both remain significant against the other four datasets individually. Excluding GaitMotion entirely leaves the result materially unchanged (confirms that one dataset isn't driving it). This is now disclosed honestly as a partial, not uniform, corroboration rather than left implicit.

**Nine major findings fixed**: (1) trunk-RMS mechanism claim (compensatory trunk lean/pelvic drop) predicted the wrong sign — stroke shows *lower* trunk RMS, not higher — revised to present a walking-speed-mediator alternative as equally plausible and untested. (2) rank-biserial sign convention now stated explicitly (positive = stroke lower than healthy), verified directly against raw medians. (3) "age-independent" placement claims corrected throughout since Felius has no demographic data to adjust for age at all. (4) a verified factual error crediting Mannini et al. (2016) with severity-grading/longitudinal-tracking removed from the Introduction. (5) the third pass's Mannini fix had over-corrected to "not genuine lumbar-placement evidence at all" — corrected again: it does use a real lumbar sensor (L4-S2), it just isn't lumbar-*dominant* evidence. (6) Inui et al. (2026)'s uneven-surface-walking protocol now disclosed alongside its accuracy figure. (7) Lee et al. (2025)'s ~37-year unaddressed stroke/healthy age gap now flagged wherever its pocket-placement result is used. (8) the Abstract/Conclusion's constructed "65.9-100% accuracy" range for classical ML removed since Section 3.7 itself says these figures aren't comparable across studies; same fix in Section 5.2. (9) the pocket-placement "reaches usable accuracy in two further included studies" claim corrected since Lee et al. (2025) reports no accuracy figure — now "remains usable, not absent." Also fixed: an unadjusted sex confound now disclosed (Voisard's HS-group gender effect was never incorporated into the primary age adjustment), four in-text citations lacking reference-list entries resolved ("as cited in Jiao et al., 2024" framing for two; explicit non-entry disclosure for two excluded screening candidates), RQ2's orientation component flagged as unanswered by available evidence, DUO-GAIT's sensor count corrected from "seven-sensor" to the accurate nine-sensor/six-site description, code-availability reporting rescoped to the 16 assessed studies with Wang et al.'s (2021) real dataset link distinguished from a bare on-request statement, and Mannini's 86.7-90.5% accuracy range given its aggregation-level caveat in Table 3.

## [2026-07-30] fix | PROSPERO statement removed; fifth journal-critic pass finds and fixes another fatal-level issue

Per direct supervisor feedback that PROSPERO registration status is not a priority for this paper, removed "No protocol was registered in advance with PROSPERO or an equivalent registry" from Section 6 and the word "prospectively registered" from a nearby comparison phrase. A fifth `journal-critic` pass then found one more fatal-level issue plus 11 major findings, all investigated by direct re-computation against the raw code (not just edited on trust) and fixed:

**FATAL, investigated and resolved**: the fourth pass's own per-healthy-dataset sensitivity check on Section 4.2.6's pooled comparison stratified only the healthy side, never the stroke side — so it couldn't detect a batch effect specific to Felius, which supplies 73% of the pooled stroke group. Direct re-computation split Voisard and Felius apart and tested each separately against the pooled healthy reference. Trunk RMS, cadence, and mean stride time hold the same direction as each dataset's own within-study result — real corroboration. Sample entropy and harmonic ratio do not: within Felius's own paired comparison, sample entropy is negligible and harmonic ratio points the opposite direction from what both show against the pooled external reference, meaning their pooled "significance" more likely reflects a between-dataset/hardware effect than pathology. Section 4.2.6 and the Conclusion were rewritten to downgrade sample entropy and harmonic ratio accordingly, keep trunk RMS/cadence/stride-time as the credible generalizing evidence, disclose the RMS channel substitutions (hip for OxWalk, foot for GaitMotion — neither has a trunk-region channel), and replace the stride-time-CV null's "dataset heterogeneity" explanation with the real one (Voisard and Felius contribute opposite-signed effects that cancel when pooled).
